PREFACE
BeginningWaray-Waray presentsabasioelementary course
infourmimeographed volumes. VolumeOnecontains thefirst
ninelessons,Volume Twothenextnine,VolumeThree the
next nine,andVolume Fourthelastthreelessons andacomplete
glossary andindexofthematerials. Thesetextsforthemost
partpresentnormal,everyday styles, thoughmore formaland
cultivated stylesarerepresentedbythereadings. Theyare
thefirsttextsevertobepublished ofnormal, coloquial
Waray andinfact, one ofthefewsourcesofWaraytextsof
anysort. Althoughtherearenearlytwo milionspeakers of
thislanguage, materialsinthelanguage oraboutthelanguage
arescanty.
Thesematerialsareintendedformembers ofthePeace
Corps, students,missionaries, diplomatsandotherswhodesire
orneedanaotive knowledge ofWaray. Accordingly,emphasis
isgiventotheconstructionsand expressions whichareoffre-
quent,everyday occurenceandwhichmakeupthe backboneof
everyday conversation. Theexercises focusonbringingthe
learnertousingtheseforms activelyandwithreadyfluency.
More formalstylesofWarayarenotignored,however.
The
learnerisintroduced totheseinthereadingselections.
Waray.
Oneoftheco-authorsisanativespeakerof
Theotherhashadtolearnitasasecondlan-
guage. Thuswehavebeenabletoidentifytheprob-
ii
===== PAGE 13 =====
iv Preface
lemswhichfacetheforeignerinlearningWaray while
atthesame timewe havebeenabletobe certainthatthe
Warayuterance presentedare realynormal.
We have also
triedtoconveyanativespeaker's feelingofwhatthese
uterances mean.
Thebasicsentences and exercises are original. The
storieswegatheredfromoralsourcesinSamar and Leyte.
Theyarepresentedastold, butwe have editedoutdialectal
peculiarities. Thesongsalsocome fromoralsources, and
wehavetriedtotranscribethem exactly astheyweresung
forus.
Theselessons couldnothave been produced withoutthe
helpofalargenumber ofpeople. Wehaveadeep debt of
gratitudetothedozens ofpeople whoentertained usinSamar
andLeyte and regaleduswithstoriesand songswithoutany
recompenseotherthanthe pleasureofhavinganaudience.
Wealsoowethankstothe Filipinos, toonumerous tomention,
whoworkedwithusinpreparing theconversations andexer-
cises.
Theselessonswereprepared under contraevbetweenthe
PeaceCorps and CornelUniversity.
Asubstantialfinancial
contributionwas alsomade bytheSoutheastAsiaProgram,
CornelUniversity. Weowethankstobothorganizationsfor
theirfinancialhelpandaspecialwordofthankstothe
personnelofthe Southeast AsiaProgramforprovidingdetailed
administrativeassistance.

INTRODUCTION
1. Waray-Waray
These lessonsprovide learningmaterialforWaray-Waray
(whichwe calhere"Waray" forshort), the language ofsome
twomilionpeople inmost ofSamar andtheEasternhalfof
Leyteinthe Philippines. Thislanguage isalso caledVisa-
yan(orBisayan), butsincethistermisalsoapplied tothe
otherlanguages intheVisayas(theislandsoftheCentral
Philippines),we prefertocalthislanguage Waray. Itis
alsoreferedtoasSamar-LeyteBisayan. InWaraythislan-
guage iscaledBinisayaq, Sinamar,LitinyuorWinaray.
TherearediferencesinWarayasitisspokenindiferent
areas. ThekindofWaray usedaroundTaclobanand Catbalogan
isthetype ofmost wide-spread use (and alsomost influential
onotherdialects), and usualythistype ofWarayisun-
derstoodinplaceswherethelocaldialectdifersstrongly
fromthistype. Itisthistype ofWaraywhichispresented
here. Importantfeatures ofotherdialectsarementioned
occasionalyinpassing, iftheirexplanation isstraight-
forwardandeasytograsp.
*
*Itisimpossible totreathighlycomplicateddialectal
featurestogetherwiththedescriptionofthestandardlan-
guage,assuchdetailswould onlyoverburden thestudent
withdetailsandhamper himinlearningthefeatures ofthe
standardlanguage. However, weretheretimeand funds, it
wouldhavebeenworthhavinganappendix and supplementary
exercises onvarious importantdialect peculiaritiesfor
thebenefitofthosestudentswhoaredestined fortheareas
wherethesepeculiaritiesareused.

xiv Introduction
2. Aim oftheCourse
Theaimoflanguage learningistousethelanguage
likeanativespeaker.
Alearnercannot, ofcourse, ina
fewmonthsduplicatetheexperiencesofawhole lifetime
ofanative speaker especialysinceasanadulthe is-
notasamenable tonewhabits asachildis.
However, he can
learntocommunicate.
Thismeans notonlythathecan
learntounderstandand saywhathewantstosayhimself,
buthecangettoknowthe subtletiesandnuanceswhich
express people's feelingsand atitudes: Inthiscourse
weaimtodevelopthestudent'sabilitytoexpresshimself
andtograsp, notonlythegistand generaltrendofthe
conversation, butthesubtletieswhichconveyatitudes
andfeelings. We aimprimarilyto giveyouafeelingfor
theimportant-thatis, frequentlyused aspectsof
Waraygrammatical structureand developyourabilityto
usethemautomaticaly. Wealsoprovide simpleexplana-
tionsandampledrilonthelitleparticlesinWaray
whichareofprime importance ingivingemotionaltone to
theuterance, theparticleswhichconveyfeelingsand
atitudesmuchasintonationdoesinEnglish. Withanau-
tomaticcontrolofgrammar and theseparticlesyouwilbe
ontheroadtocomplete understandingand fulexpression
ofthesenuances.
3. Achievement
Thefirsteighteenlessonscoverthe basicprinciples

4. Make-up oftheLessons
Eachlessonconsistsofasectionofbasic sentences tobe
memorized, exercises onthebasic sentences (questions, patern

InadditionLessons 19
through29 containareadingselectionwithcommentary and

choicequestions, completionexercises, compositionexercises
andgrammar exercises). Tapesareprovidedforalbasic sen-
tences, questions and paternpractices.

6. DispositionoftheClassTime
Length
Classes shouldnotlastmore than45minutestoanhour.

Afterthistimethestudentsshouldbegivenashortbreak,
and aftertwo classes alongerrecessisnecessary
Schedule ofanOrdinaryDay
Itisnotrecommended thatclassesbeheldformore
thanfivehoursaday.
Formoststudentsfourhourswith
anequalamount ofstudytimeathome givethebestresults.
Firsthour: Quickrepetitionofsentences memorized
forthatday; testofmemorization; read over sentences to
bememorizedforfolowingday.
Secondhour: Exercisesonthebasic sentences(ques-
tions, patern practicesand freeconversation).
Thirdhour:Grammatical explanations, answeringof
studentsquestions, exercises and patern practicesonthe
grammar.
Fourthhour: Exercises and patern practices, free
conversation, answerquestions onbasic sentences orread-
ings.
Ifthere isafifthhour, the fifthhourshouldbe of
the same character asthesecondand fourth.
Discipline

grammatical explanations arebeinggiven). Alother

Language
STICKTOWARAY. NotawordofEnglishshouldbespoken
fromthefirstminute ofthefirstday totheveryend. The
proceduressetupintheselessonsdonotalowfortheuse
ofEnglish, and assoonasyouuseEnglishyouaredoing

anyquestions withwhichthey have beenbotheredand the

itcandispensewiththegrammatical explanations, andprefers

hearingandspeakingWaray, althebeter. Theexplanations
inthebookaremeant tobe complete, and ifthe studentscan
makedowithno furtherdiscussion, thenmore timeisleft
forhearingand practicingthelanguage.

Grammatical Section
1.1 Pronunciation
Everylanguagehasitsown setofsounds whichisdiferent
fromthatofeveryotherlanguage. Notwolanguagesarethesame
===== PAGE 52 =====
GrammaticalSection 1-13
ineverydetailoftheirsound systems. Thus,whenonelearnsanew
language,hemust formthehabitofpronouncing newsounds andusing
themautomaticaly. Thehabitisnotformed withoutconscious efort.
whathehears:
Thestudentimitates hasitrepeatedagain; andimitatesagain,
A
tryingtocome closer. Thehabitofmaking newsounds develops ashe
1
repeatstheprocess hundredsoftimes,uptothepointthathehimself
knows- withoutcomparingwhathehassaidwithanativespeaker's
model-whetherornotitsoundsright. Goodpronunciationresults
fromanawarenessofwhatthesoundsofthelanguagearelikeand
constantpracticeinmaking themcorectly.

1.21 Droppingofq(glotalstop)
Innormalspeech, theglotalstopisdropped exceptbeforea
pause andafteraconsonant. Thus,Tubiggadlagitákunkarúyag
'Iwouldjustliketohavewater,
'isusualypronounced,Túbiggad
latakkarúvag (1.12). Thegoflaghasbeendroppedentirely, and
ákunhasbeenshortened toak.
1.22 Dropping ofsylables
Thefinalsylableofcommonwordsisoftendropped: ákun'my'
isshortened toak, etc. (SeethechartinSec.1.31.)
1.23 Coalescence ofvowels
Twovowelsseparatedbyaglotalstopinslowspeechareoften
coalesced inrapidspeech whentheglotalstopisdropped. Ifone
ofthevowolsisi,iisdropped. Thuslagitispronouncedlat:

water.!

1.31 Kami vs.kita
Kami (námun,nam.etc)means 'we' or'us' ifthepersonspoken to
isnotincluded:
Mákadtunakami.

Maydaqpakami kákadtúan.

Westilhave togosome place

shortened tot.

Anappositive (a phrasewhichisput

2.12 diniyan
Thefinalsylableofdinhihasbeen
dropped.andthetwon'scolapsed
intoone.

fortheplural. The postposed formisniplusthename for

thepersonaddressed, butnotnearthespeaker;andfarfrom
boththepersonspeaking orthepersonaddressed.

2.51 Linkingofthedemonstratives
Thedemonstrative pronounisusualy linkedwithnga*
tothewordfolowing it.
Hinqu ini(adi, itu, adtu) ngabátaq?'Who isthis
(thishere, that, thatthere)child?'
Kadákuq hini(hitu) ngabátaq!'How bigthis(that)
childis!
Ifthedemonstrative areshortened the linkerisstil
used:
Hinquit(ad,in,at) ngabátaq? Who isthat
(thishere,this,thatthere)child?'
Kadákuq hitngabátaq! 'How bigthatchildis!'

fromthesubjectmarkerit(2.2)bytheuse ofalinker:

yours?'

Who isyourfriend?'

'this'canbedistinguished fromthegenitivemarkers hit

3.5,3.6 pak
Thisisacontractionforpaand ak.
3.7
ngada, la
Notethatthegofngadagand laqis
dropped. Speakers frommostparts
ofLeyte tend todropthefinalgof
wordsinthemiddle ofthesentence,
butasaruletheypronounce the
finalqifitoccursatthe end of
g
thelastwordofthesentence.

isexpressed byhan[plustime]'
(Sec. 11.3).

4.12 Theuseofthedependentformsinconditional clauses
referingtofuturetime
Inconditionalclausesreferingtofuturetime, 'if
[so-and-so]happens, were tohappen,' thedependentformof
theverbisused:
Kunumuranngániq,ayawpagqupud haíya.
'Ifit
should rain,donotgowithhim.
'
Thewordkun'if' is, however,notinvariablyfolowedby
adependent form. Incaseswhenkunistranslatedby'when'
(e.g.kunnáuran'whenitrains,' kunmákanhi hiva'whenhe
comes, etc.)aformoftheverbotherthanthedependentform
isused.
Similarlyincaseswhentheverbreferstoan
intentionalaction(e.g.kuntiuliqhiya'ifheplanstocome
home,
'etc.), theintentionalverbformisused.

4.41 Kain,ngainandverbsformed fromthem
Kain, ngainformverbsmeaning'where?' Kinainmeans
'where did[subject] goto?'
KinainhiIntuy.
'
Where didIntoygoto?'
'
Nakainmeans'wherewas?
NákainhiIntuy?
'
WherewasIntoy?'
**The prefixesna-andma-withtheseverbsisnotfoundin
aldialects.Theyareusedinmost placesinLeyte.

Lesson4

Makain(orngain)hiIntuy? WhereisIntoygoingto?'
Tikainmeans'where is[subject]intendingtogoto?'
Tikainka? '
Whereareyougoingto?'

Commentary toBasicSentences
5.1 kita
Whentalkingaboutthingsgeneraly
applicable toeveryoneonerefers
tohimselfaskita'we' ratherthan
aku'I.! Not'Where doIgeta
jeepforTacloban?,'but, Wheredo
weget..?'
dyiptikadtu
Notethatthelinkerngais
dropped. Thisusage isveryrare.
Thengamaybe droppedbefore
phrasesmeaning'goingto..;
'coming from..
'
5.2 hitunga iskina'Atthatcorner.
' Inphrases
introducedbyademonstrative
which refertolocationthegeni-
tiveformisused:hitu'at,in
that;' hini'at, inthis;'hadtu
'at,inthat;' hadi'at, inthis.
5.4 hitngakalsáda 'Atthatstreet.
Sec.5.2,above.
' Seethecomment
5.11itun'It.
'
The demonstrative isoften
usedinWaraywhenwe use'it'
inEnglish.

Questions onBasicSentences 5-9
5.12 díriqmaíha'Won'ttake long.
'
Thisformis
dialectal. The more widespread
usage ismaggíha.
5.13 ukuy
Notethatukuy means both'stop'
and live.
'
5.20 hinpagqabut Hin,
'When [so-and-so]arives.
'
hitplusanabstractmeans'when
[so-and-so]happens.
' Hanplus
anabstractmeans 'whenTso-and-
so]happened.
'

5.2 Existentialsentences
AnEXISTENTIAL SENTENCE isasentencewhichafirmsor
deniestheexistence ofsomethingorwhichsaysthatsomebody
hasordoes nothavea[such-and-such]:
Maykukutsi.
'Ihave acar.
'
Warayaku kutsi.
'Idon'thavea car.
'
Waray lubiha ámun.
ourplace.
'
Therearenococonuts in
Afirmativeexistentialsentences (thosethatafirmthe
existenceofsomething)containtheword may ormaygádaq
(alternativelypronounced maydag ormaygadaq). There isno
diference inmeaning between maygádaqandmay:
===== PAGE 250 =====

tenceofsomething)containwaray:
'
'

Wehave no car.

'Therearenococonuts here.
5.21 Theuseofthenominative andgenitive inexistential
sentences
Inexistential sentences containingmay,mayqádag, or
waray,theagent(theonewho has somethingorother)is
expressedalternativelybyanominative formoragenitive
form. There isno diference inmeaning between existential
sentences withanominative and existentialsentenceswith
agenitive:
Mayqadaq námun (orkami)balayha Taklúban.
'We have ahouse inTacloban.
'
Warayniya(orhiya)balaydidtuhaTaklúban.
'He doesn'thave ahouse inTacloban.
'
May kutsihi(orni) Intuy.
'Intoyhas acar.
'
Mayqádaqhira(ornira) sangkay haAmirika.
'Theyhave afriendinAmerica.
'

oftheverbhappened) isanominativeformofthepronoun,a
propernounpreceded byhioracommon nounpreceded byan,
it(itun)orin.

9.14 Whentochoosetheactiveorpassiveifeitherformis
possible
Inmany cases thereisachoiceofwhether tousethe
activeorpassive (justinEnglishwe may sayeither'Mozart
wrotetheJupiterSymphony' or'TheJupiterSymphony was
writenbyIlozart'). InWaray theactiveisusedtotel
whatsomebody orsomething does; the passiveisusedtotel
aboutsomething orsomebody whathappenstohimorwhat-
somebody didtohim. Thediferenceisone ofemphasis:
withactiveverbsatentionisdirectedtotheactionitself,
andtheagent isinfocus; withpassiveverbsatentionis
directedtotheperson towhom orthingtowhichtheaction
happens, andtheperson orthingisinfocus. Inmany sen-
tencestheatentionisfocusedontherecipient oftheaction.
Thisfactoraccountsforthehighfrequencyofsentences with
passiveverbsinWarayascontrasted withEnglish.
Inthe
folowingexamples, theactiveisusedinthefirstsentence
ofeachpair, the passive, inthe second:

9A. DirectPassiveVerbForms (Sec.9.1f).
9A1.PaternPractice
Step1. Liwanliwan:
Liwanliwan: 'IwaitedforIntuy.
'
1. ÁkunginhulathiIntuy.
2. AkunginhúhulathiIntuy.
